Collaborative Yet Personalized Policy Training: Single-Timescale Federated Actor-Critic
Leo Muxing Wang, Pengkun Yang, Lili Su

Abstract
Despite the popularity of the actor-critic method and the practical needs of collaborative policy training, existing works typically either overlook environmental heterogeneity or give up personalization altogether by training a single shared policy across all agents. We consider a federated actor-critic framework in which agents share a common linear subspace representation while maintaining personalized local policy components, and agents iteratively estimate the common subspace, local critic heads, and local policies (i.e., actors). Under canonical single-timescale updates with Markovian sampling, we establish finite-time convergence via a novel joint linear approximation framework. Specifically, we show that the critic error converges to zero at the rate of 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 / ( ( 1 − γ ) 4 ​ T ​ K ) ) \widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/((1-\gamma)^{4}\sqrt{TK})) , and the policy gradient norm converges to zero at the rate of 𝒪 ~ ​ ( 1 / ( ( 1 − γ ) 6 ​ T ​ K ) ) \widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/((1-\gamma)^{6}\sqrt{TK})) , where T T is the number of rounds, K K is the number of agents, and γ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \gamma\in(0,1) is the discount factor. These results demonstrate linear speedup with respect to the number of agents K K , despite heterogeneous Markovian trajectories under distinct transition kernels and coupled learning dynamics. To address these challenges, we develop a new perturbation analysis for the projected subspace updates and QR decomposition steps, together with conditional mixing arguments for heterogeneous Markovian noise. Furthermore, to handle the additional complications induced by policy updates and temporal dependence, we establish fine-grained characterizations of the discrepancies between function evaluations under Markovian sampling and under temporally frozen policies. Experiments instantiate the framework within PPO on federated Hopper-v5 action-map heterogeneity, showing gains over Single PPO and FedAvg PPO and downstream transfer from the learned shared trunk.



1
Introduction

Collaborative yet personalized policy training is becoming increasingly important in modern reinforcement learning systems, particularly in emerging Embodied AI applications, where the state and action spaces are highly complex and large-scale.
Enabling agents to collaborate can substantially improve sample efficiency and reduce training costs, while personalization remains essential for adapting to environment-specific characteristics and avoiding performance degradation caused by misaligned learning signals.
The growing demand for simultaneously achieving collaboration and personalization is also reflected in supervised learning, where personalized federated learning (PFL) has achieved remarkable empirical and theoretical success by effectively exploiting commonality while adapting to heterogeneity in local data distributions
8
;
50
;
24
;
36
;
23
;
10
;
13
;
16
.

However, training personalized policies in federated reinforcement learning remains largely underexplored.
Existing works typically either overlook environmental heterogeneity or give up personalization altogether by training a single shared policy across all agents.
Yang et al.
52
studied the training of personalized policies in the context where each agent has a private reward function but shares a common transition kernel for the environment. The analysis focused solely on the convergence of the global policy, defined as the average of the local policies.
Jin et al.
18
proposed a heuristic deep FedRL method, where the policy network can be decomposed into a shared subnetwork and environment embedding layer.
Xiong et al.
49
studied personalized TD learning with two timescales and established a linear convergence speedup.
Environmental heterogeneity was also considered in
(
18
;
42
;
47
;
53
)
, which mostly focused on training a common policy or value-/Q-function. Moreover, their notions of optimality are often characterized with respect to an imaginary averaged environment constructed from weighted combinations of the agents’ transition kernels
18
.

Actor–critic methods have emerged as a widely adopted reinforcement learning framework and have been successfully applied across a range of practical domains. By jointly performing gradient-based policy updates to maximize cumulative rewards while simultaneously learning value function estimates under the current policy, actor–critic methods effectively balance exploration and evaluation. This coupled structure enables the critic to provide low-variance feedback to the actor, leading to more stable and sample-efficient learning.
Despite this empirical success, finite-time analysis of actor–critic algorithms has only recently begun to emerge
28
;
6
;
5
, and remains significantly less understood in multi-agent and heterogeneous environments.

Contributions.

In this paper,
we consider a federated actor-critic framework in which agents share a common linear subspace representation while maintaining personalized local policy components. In this setting, agents collaboratively estimate the shared subspace while simultaneously updating their local critic heads and personalized policies (i.e., actors). In particular, we focus on the canonical single-timescale update scheme under Markovian sampling, which is both practically appealing and analytically more challenging.
Our contributions can be summarized as follows.

•

We propose a single-timescale personalized federated AC algorithm (
pFedAC
) that enables the
K
federated agents to collaboratively train policies for maximizing their own cumulative rewards.
To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to enable personalized AC in federated reinforcement learning.

•

We establish finite-time convergence via a novel joint linear approximation framework.
We show that the critic error converges to zero at the rate of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/((1-\gamma)^{4}\sqrt{TK}))
, and the policy gradient norm converges to zero at the rate of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/((1-\gamma)^{6}\sqrt{TK}))
, where
T
is the number of rounds,
K
is the number of agents, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
is the discount factor. Here
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}
hides polylogarithmic factors, absolute constants, and fixed problem-dependent Lipschitz constants.
These results demonstrate linear speedup with respect to the number of agents
K
.
The dependence on
(1-\gamma)
may not be optimal, and we would like to leave this as a future direction.

•

Different from existing finite-time analyses of actor–critic methods, our framework requires controlling three tightly coupled error dynamics. In particular, the shared subspace updates and heterogeneous local transition kernels necessitate a careful exploitation of the underlying geometric structures to establish convergence.
Moreover, departing from traditional PFL analyses, our setting observes heterogeneous Markovian trajectories under distinct transition kernels. To address these challenges, we develop a new perturbation analysis for the projected subspace updates and QR decomposition steps, together with conditional mixing arguments for heterogeneous Markovian noise.
To handle the additional complications introduced by policy updates and Markovian dependence, we further develop fine-grained characterizations of the discrepancies between functions evaluated under Markovian sampling and under temporally frozen policies.

•

We instantiate the proposed shared-representation and personalized-head structure within PPO and evaluate it on a federated
Hopper-v5
benchmark with action-map heterogeneity. The results show that personalization outperforms Single PPO and FedAvg PPO, and that the learned shared trunk can accelerate downstream adaptation with only newly initialized heads trained.

Due to space limitations, a formal related work section is deferred to Appendix
C
.

2
Preliminaries

Markov decision process.

Before introducing the federated setting, we first provide preliminaries on the single-agent policy gradient and actor–critic (AC) algorithms. An agent is modelled as a Markov Decision Process (MDP)
{\mathcal{M}}=<P,{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}},R,\gamma>
, where
{\mathcal{S}}
is the (possibly infinite) state space,

{\mathcal{A}}
is a finite action space,
\gamma\in(0,1)
is the discount factor,

P
is the transition kernel that governs the state transition distribution given
(s,a)
,
and
R
is the reward function:
{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{A}}\rightarrow[-U_{r},U_{r}]
.

Let the policy
\pi
be parameterized by
\theta
.
The value function is defined as

\displaystyle V^{\pi_{\theta}}(s)={\mathbb{E}}_{\pi_{\theta},P}\bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}R(s_{t},a_{t})\mid s_{0}=s\bigg],

(1)

and the action-value function is

\displaystyle Q^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)

\displaystyle=R(s,a)+\gamma{\mathbb{E}}_{s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid(s,a))}[V^{\pi_{\theta}}(s^{\prime})],

(2)

where
(s_{t},a_{t})
is the state-action pair at iteration
t
for
t=0,1,\cdots
.
The expectation in Eq. (
1
) and Eq.(
2
) is taken over the state-action trajectories generated under the Markov chain that is jointly determined by the policy
\pi_{\theta}
and the transition kernels
P
.

The reinforcement learning tasks typically aim to find a policy
\pi_{\theta}
that maximizes the cumulative discounted rewards averaged over the given initial state distribution
\eta(s)
:

J(\theta)={\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\eta}\left[V^{\pi_{\theta}}(s)\right],

(3)

For ease of exposition, we write
V^{\pi_{\theta}}(s)
and
Q^{\pi_{\theta}}(s,a)
as
V_{\theta}(s)
and
Q_{\theta}(s,a)
, respectively.

We assume that the limiting state distribution induced by the transition kernel
P
under policy
\pi_{\theta}
exists. Define the
discounted state visitation distribution
under policy
\pi_{\theta}
as

\nu_{\theta}(s)=(1-\gamma){\mathbb{E}}_{s_{0}\sim\eta}\bigg[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}p_{\theta,t}(s|s_{0})\bigg],

where
s_{0}
denotes initial state, and
p_{\theta,t}(s|s_{0})
is the probability that
s_{t}=s
given the initial state is
s_{0}
under policy
\pi_{\theta}
. The cumulative discounted reward
J(\theta)
can be rewritten as
35
:

\displaystyle J(\theta)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\nu_{\theta},a\sim\pi_{\theta}}[R(s,a)].

For ease of exposition, we write
{\mathbb{E}}_{s\sim\nu_{\theta},a\sim\pi_{\theta}}[R(s,a)]
as
{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{\theta}\times\pi_{\theta}}[R(s,a)]
for short.

Policy gradient theorem.

For parameterized
\pi_{\theta}
, one natural idea is to mimic the traditional optimization framework and run gradient ascent to maximize the cumulative discounted reward as defined in Eq.(
3
), assuming it is differentiable.
The policy gradient theorem
34
provides an analytic expression for the gradient:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\theta}}[Q_{\theta}(s,a)\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)].

(4)

Note that for any function
h:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
that is independent of the action, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}h(s)\nabla_{\theta}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid s)=h(s)\nabla_{\theta}\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\pi_{\theta}(a\mid s)=h(s)\nabla_{\theta}1=0,~~~\text{for any $s\in{\mathcal{S}}$}.

Thus, choosing
h(\cdot)=V_{\theta}(\cdot)
,
\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}J(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}{\mathbb{E}}_{\nu_{\theta}\otimes\pi_{\theta}}[(Q_{\theta}(s,a)-V_{\theta}(s))\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)],

where
(Q_{\theta}(s,a)-V_{\theta}(s))
is often called the advantage function.
In practice, the
Q_{\theta}
and
V_{\theta}
are often unknown and must be approximated in an online manner. Traditional approaches, such as Monte Carlo–based methods, suffer from high variance and thus learn slowly in practice
6
;
28
.

Actor-critic algorithm.

By jointly performing gradient-based policy updates while simultaneously learning
Q_{\theta}
, the AC methods effectively balance exploration and evaluation, providing lower-variance feedback to the actor and thereby promoting faster convergence
20
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a function space that
V_{\theta}
belongs to.
Let
\phi:{\mathcal{S}}\to L^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
be a feature map of
{\mathcal{V}}
.
Denote the coefficients of the value
V_{\theta}
with respect to
\phi
as
z_{\theta}^{*}
, where
z_{\theta}^{*}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{N}})
. For simplicity, we assume
\phi
is known and has finite dimension
d
, i.e.,
\phi:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Consequently,

\displaystyle V_{\theta}(s)=\left\langle\phi(s),z_{\theta}^{*}\right\rangle,~~~~\forall~s\in{\mathcal{S}}.

(5)

This corresponds to the standard linear function approximation setting commonly adopted in the reinforcement learning literature
17
;
33
;
29
. More generally, one may allow for approximation error by writing

V_{\theta}(s)=\left\langle\phi(s),z_{\theta}^{*}\right\rangle+\epsilon,~~~~\forall~s\in{\mathcal{S}},

where
\epsilon
captures the model misspecification. In this paper, we assume Eq.(
5
) for ease of exposition. Our analysis readily extends to the more general case.

In practice,
V_{\theta}
is unknown.
We consider the linear function approximation
\widehat{V}(s;z)=\phi(s)^{\top}z
to
V_{\theta}
.
For any given
(s,a,s^{\prime})
, where
s^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid s,a)
, we use the one-step temporal difference error (i.e., TD(0) error) to estimate the advantage function, i.e,

\delta:=R(s,a)+\left(\gamma\phi(s^{\prime})-\phi(s)\right)^{\top}z,

which in turn yields a stochastic gradient–type value function and policy updates:

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\alpha\delta\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s),\qquad\text{and}\qquad z^{\prime}=z+\beta\delta\phi(s),

where
\alpha,\beta>0
are the stepsizes.
It is well-known that under some standard technical assumptions
\lim_{t\to\infty}z_{t}\to z_{\theta}^{*}
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\nabla J(\theta_{t})\|=0

(
40
;
34
;
20
)
.

3
Problem Formulation

We consider a federated system that consists of one parameter server and
K
agents.
The agents are modelled as Markov Decision Processes (MDP)
\{{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\mid{\mathcal{M}}^{k}=<{\mathcal{S}},{\mathcal{A}},\gamma,P^{k},R^{k}>\text{ for }k=1,2,...,K\}
.
The environments
P^{k}
with which the agents interact are heterogeneous across agents.
Let
J^{k}(\theta)
,
V_{\theta}^{k}(s)
, and
Q_{\theta}^{k}(s,a)
denote, respectively, the cumulative reward, the value function, and the action-value (
Q
) function under policy
\pi_{\theta}
, where the expectations are taken with respect to
\pi_{\theta}
and the local transition kernel
P^{k}
.

The parameter server can orchestrate the learning at the agents through iteratively aggregating the updates at the agents. The
K
agents aim to collaboratively train
personalized
policies
\pi_{\theta^{*}}^{k}
’s that maximize their cumulative rewards
J^{k}(\theta)
, respectively.
Learning with shared representation is widely recognized as an effective way to separate commonalities from heterogeneity

pFedAC

1:

Input:
Initial heads
\{\omega_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
; initial subspace
{\mathbf{B}}_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
, which is orthonormal; stepsizes:
\beta
for local heads,
\zeta
for subspace,
\gamma
for reward estimator, and
\alpha
for actor; total iteration
T
; projection upper bound
U_{\omega}
such that
\|\omega^{k,*}\|\leq U_{\omega}
for
k\in[K]
.

2:

for

k=1,2,\dots,K

do

3:

Draw
s_{0,0}^{k}\sim\eta
, and
\widehat{s}_{0,0}^{k}\sim\eta
.

4:

end

for

5:

for

t=0,1,2,\dots,T-1

do

6:

for

k=1,2,\dots,K

do

7:

Sample
\{s_{t,0}^{k},a_{t,0}^{k},\dots,s_{t,L}^{k}\}\sim\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}}\otimes P^{k}
.

8:

\delta_{t,L}^{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}(r_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma\phi(s_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,\ell}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k})
.

9:

Sample
\{\widehat{s}_{t,0}^{k},\widehat{a}_{t,0}^{k},\dots,\widehat{s}_{t,L}^{k}\}\sim\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}}\otimes\widehat{P}^{k}
.

10:

\delta_{t,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}=\widehat{r}_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}
for
\ell=0,\ldots,L-1
.

11:

\omega^{k}_{t+1}=\Pi_{U_{\omega}}\left(\omega^{k}_{t}+\beta\frac{1}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\right)
.

.

13:

\theta_{t+1}^{k}=\theta_{t}^{k}+\alpha\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\delta_{t,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}}(\widehat{a}_{t,\ell}^{k}|\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}^{k})
.

14:

s_{t+1,0}^{k}=s_{t,L}^{k}
, and
\widehat{s}_{t+1,0}^{k}=\widehat{s}_{t,L}^{k}
.

15:

end

for

16:

\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}
.

17:

\mathbf{B}_{t+1},\mathbf{R}_{t+1}=\texttt{QR}(\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1})
.

18:

Server broadcasts
\mathbf{B}_{t+1}
to all agents.

19:

end

for

across various heterogeneous data
4
;
1
;
2
;
22
;
27
.
Inspired by the literature of personalized FL and multi-task learning,
following
39
;
8
;
37
;
12
;
11
;
38
;
27
,
we assume that
\{z^{k,*}:~k=1,\cdots,K\}
– the best linear approximation coefficients under
\phi(s)
– belong to and fully span an
r
-dimensional subspace. For ease of exposition, and without significant loss of generality, we assume
\epsilon=0
.
Formally, assume that there exists an orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{B}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times r}
and
\omega_{\theta}^{k,*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
such that

\displaystyle z_{\theta}^{k,*}=\mathbf{B}^{*}\omega_{\theta}^{k,*},~~~\forall~\theta.

(6)

4
Algorithm: Personalized Single-Timescale Federated AC

We propose a single-timescale personalized federated AC algorithm (
pFedAC
) that enables the
K
agents to collaboratively train policies for maximizing their own cumulative rewards
J^{k}(\theta)
.
Inspired by the seminal work
8
in federated learning, our proposed approach
exploits the shared underlying structure across agents while mitigating negative knowledge transfer through a joint linear
approximation framework, in which agents iteratively estimate the common latent subspace
\mathbf{B}^{*}
together with their respective local “head” parameters
\omega_{\theta}^{k,*}
.

To mitigate the mismatch between the limiting state distribution involved in the critic updates and the discounted state visitation distribution involved in the policy gradient, following
7
, we employ a simulator to generate auxiliary state-action pairs for the policy updates.
A formal description can be found in Algorithm
1
, which we explain next.

Critic.

At a high level, in each iteration, each agent locally performs the TD(
L
) update, and uses the obtained TD(
L
) error
\delta_{t,L}^{k}
– formally defined in Eq. (
7
) – and rewards to update estimates of its local head
\omega^{k}
and common subspace
{\mathbf{B}}
. A similar TD(
L
) scheme was also considered in
(
49
, Algorithm 2)
, which studied personalization through shared feature representations.

Intuitively, the updates of
\omega^{k}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
can be interpreted as performing a perturbed stochastic gradient on the following joint linear approximation problem:

\min_{{\mathbf{B}},\omega^{1},\cdots,\omega^{K}}~\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\theta}^{k}}[(\widehat{V}^{k}(s;\omega^{k},\mathbf{B})-V_{\theta}^{k}(s))^{2}],

where
\widehat{V}^{k}(s;\omega^{k},\mathbf{B})=\phi(s)^{\top}\mathbf{B}{\omega^{k}}.
Since
V^{k}
is unknown, a true stochastic gradient cannot be computed directly.
Following the semi-gradient method TD(
L
), we approximate the stochastic gradients w.r.t.
\omega
and
\mathbf{B}
using the temporal-difference (TD) error, defined as

\displaystyle\delta_{t,L}^{k}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}{\omega_{t}^{k}},

(7)

where we write
r_{t,\ell}^{k}=R(s_{t,\ell}^{k},a_{t,\ell}^{k})
for ease of exposition.
Thus,

\displaystyle\omega_{t+1}^{k}=\prod\nolimits_{U_{\omega}}(\omega_{t}^{k}+\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}_{t}}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})),~~\text{and}~~\mathbf{B}_{t+1}^{k}=\mathbf{B}_{t}+\frac{\zeta}{L}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top},

(8)

where
\prod\nolimits_{w}
denotes the projection operator onto the
\ell_{2}
ball of radius at most
U_{\omega}
.

Remark 1

(On the Benefits of Projection)
.

In Eq.(
8
), the updates of both
\omega
and
{\mathbf{B}}
involve (different) projection operations.
The projection operation on
\omega
gives us a natural coarse bound
U_{\omega}
, while the innovation projection in the update of
{\mathbf{B}}
substantially suppresses perturbation amplification from cross terms; in particular, it ensures that the QR decomposition perturbation remains second order.

Although projection operations are not necessary in several existing analyses for single-agent or federated RL with a common policy
19
;
33
;
25
,
our setting involves tightly coupled dynamics among the shared subspace
{\mathbf{B}}
, local critic heads
\omega^{k}
, and personalized policies
\theta^{k}
, together with heterogeneous transition kernels
P^{k}
. Consequently, establishing a tractable finite-time analysis requires combining fine-grained perturbation arguments with coarse uniform bounds induced by the projection operators.
Without relying on any absolute bound on
\omega^{k}
, characterizing the error dynamics quickly becomes intractable.

Actor.

Following
7
;
15
, for the actor updates at each agent, we sample from a companion MDP with transition kernel
\widehat{P}^{k}=\gamma P^{k}+(1-\gamma)\eta
– recalling that
\eta
is the given initial state distribution. That is, with probability
\gamma
, the next state is generated according to
P^{k}
; otherwise, it is drawn from the initial state distribution
\eta
.
Formally, in each round
t
, the trajectory
\{\widehat{s}_{t,0}^{k},\widehat{a}_{t,0}^{k},\widehat{r}_{t,0}^{k},\dots,\widehat{s}_{t,L}^{k}\}
for the actor update is generated as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{t,\ell}\sim~\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}}(\cdot|~\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}),~\text{and}~~\widehat{s}_{t,\ell+1}\sim~\widehat{P}^{k}(\cdot|~\widehat{s}_{t,\ell},\widehat{a}_{t,\ell}),~~\text{where}~\widehat{P}^{k}=\gamma P^{k}+(1-\gamma)\eta

(9)

for
\ell=0,\cdots,L-1
.
For the actor update, we also define the one-step TD error

\displaystyle\delta_{t,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}=\widehat{r}_{t,\ell}^{k}+\left(\gamma\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}^{k})\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k},\qquad\ell=0,\ldots,L-1.

(10)

where
\widehat{r}_{t,\ell}^{k}=R^{k}(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell},\widehat{a}_{t,\ell})
,
\beta
and
\zeta
are stepsizes of
\omega^{k}
and
{\mathbf{B}}
, respectively.
We use the length-
L
trajectory as a minibatch of one-step TD policy-gradient estimators, leading to the update rule

\displaystyle\theta_{t+1}^{k}=\theta_{t}^{k}+\alpha\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\delta_{t,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}\nabla_{\theta}\log\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}}(\widehat{a}_{t,\ell}^{k}|\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}^{k}).

Note that the above Markovian sampling procedure generally requires access to a simulator whose state can be reset arbitrarily. This approach also enjoys several favorable properties that facilitate accurate estimation of the policy gradient.

Proposition 1
.

49

For the actor Markov chain in Eq.(
9
), we have

d_{TV}(\mathbb{P}(\widehat{s}_{t}\mid s_{0}),\nu_{\theta})\leq\gamma^{t}
, for all
t\geq 0
and
s_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

5
Convergence Analysis

We present some technical assumptions adopted in our convergence analysis.
Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
are widely adopted in the RL literature.
Assumption
5
quantifies how well spread the underlying truth
z_{\theta}^{k,*}
is in covering the
r
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
.

Assumption 1

(Exploration)
.

The matrix
A_{L,\theta}^{k}=\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[\phi(s^{(0)})(\gamma^{L}\phi(s^{(L)})-\phi(s^{(0)}))^{\top}],

where the expectation is taken with respect to
s^{(0)}\sim\mu_{\theta}^{k},(a^{(\ell)},s^{(\ell+1)})\sim\pi_{\theta}\otimes P^{k}~\forall\ell\in[0,L-1]
, is negative definite and its largest eigenvalue is upper bounded by
-L\lambda
.

This assumption is widely adopted in analyzing TD learning with linear function approximation
6
;
3
;
5
;
28
;
29
;
46
;
58
.
Intuitively,
A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}
captures the exploration of the policy
\pi
under the transition kernel
P^{k}
within
L
steps. For the tabular setting, Assumption
1
holds when the policy
\pi
explores all state-action pairs with
L
steps.

Assumption 2

(Uniform ergodicity and one-step contraction)
.

For any policy parameter
\theta
, let

{\mathcal{K}}_{\theta}^{k}(s,ds^{\prime}):=\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}\pi_{\theta}(a|s)P^{k}(ds^{\prime}|s,a)

be the induced state-transition kernel, and let
{\mathbb{P}}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s)
denote the state distribution after
\tau
steps given
s_{0}^{k}=s
for agent
k
. There exists
\rho\in(0,1)
, uniformly over
k\in[K]
and
\theta
, such that

d_{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s),\mu_{\theta}^{k}(\cdot))\leq\rho^{\tau},~\forall\tau\geq 0,s\in{\mathcal{S}},

(11)

where
d_{TV}
is the total variation distance. Moreover,

d_{TV}(\nu_{1}{\mathcal{K}}_{\theta}^{k},\nu_{2}{\mathcal{K}}_{\theta}^{k})\leq\rho d_{TV}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

for any two probability measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
on
{\mathcal{S}}
, i.e., satisfying one-step Dobrushin contraction.

In our analysis, with a little abuse of notation, we also use
{\mathbb{P}}_{0:\tau}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot|s_{0}^{k}=s)
, which denotes the state-action pair at time
\tau
given
s_{0}^{k}=s
.
Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{k}
is the limiting distribution induced by policy
\pi_{\theta}
and the transition kernel
P^{k},\forall k
. Uniform mixing conditions are often assumed to characterize the noise induced by Markovian sampling
(
6
;
28
;
58
;
46
)
. The Dobrushin contraction part is the stronger one-step condition used below to compare the original and auxiliary chains.

Assumption 3
.

For any state-action pair
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log\pi_{\theta}(a|s)\|\leq B,~~\forall\theta;

\displaystyle\|\nabla\log\pi_{\theta_{1}}(a|s)-\nabla\log\pi_{\theta_{2}}(a|s)\|\leq L_{g}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,~~~~\forall~\theta_{1},\theta_{2};

\displaystyle|\pi_{\theta_{1}}(a|s)-\pi_{\theta_{2}}(a|s)|\leq L_{\pi}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,~~~~\forall~\theta_{1},\theta_{2}.

Assumption
3
is standard in the literature and holds for policy classes, such as Gaussian and softmax.

Assumption 4
.

\|\phi(s)\|\leq 1
for each
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

This assumption is rather standard in RL literature
35
;
34
;
6
;
28
;
58
;
46
.
The existence of a shared common subspace helps to reduce the local learning problem from a
d
-dimensional problem to an
r
-dimensional problem. In PFL, when
r\ll d
, these benefits are well characterized and are indeed substantial in derived bounds
8
;
27
. Under Assumption
4
, however, the dependence on
d
is masked, potentially obscuring these gains in the resulting bounds. Adopting more fine-grained assumptions could therefore lead to significantly sharper results. Developing such refinements, however, would require a fundamental rethinking of the problem and is left for future work.
Let

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times K},~~~\text{with }z_{t}^{k,*}~~\text{as the $k$-th column},

(12)

where
z_{t}^{k,*}
is the shorthand for
z^{k,*}(\theta_{t}^{k})
.
By Eq. (
6
), we know that the rank of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top}
is
r
. We require that each of the
r
dimensions of
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
is well-covered by
\{z_{t}^{k,*}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, formally stated in Assumption
5
.

Assumption 5
.

\frac{1}{K}\lambda^{+}_{\min}({\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top})\geq\nu
for some
\nu>0
.

We also use
\lambda_{\min}^{+}=\lambda_{\min}^{+}({\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top})
to denote the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top}
.

5.1
Main Convergence Results

In Algorithm
1
, three groups of variables are updated: the common subspace estimate
{\mathbf{B}}_{t}
, the local heads
\omega_{t}^{k}
, and the actor parameters
\theta_{t}^{k}
. In this subsection, we characterize their evolutions over time. For ease of exposition, we introduce a set of notation:

\displaystyle x_{t}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{B}}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}-z_{t}^{k,*},\qquad m_{t}={\mathbf{B}}^{*\top}_{\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t},

(13)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}=\omega^{k}_{t}+\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\mathbf{B}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}),

(14)

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-z_{t+1}^{k,*},\quad\quad\bar{m}_{t+1}={\mathbf{B}}^{*\top}_{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}.

(15)

Fix
\tau
.
For any
t\geq\tau
, define

M_{t}={\mathbb{E}}\|m_{t}\|_{F}^{2},\quad\bar{X}_{t}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbb{E}}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2},\quad\bar{G}_{t}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{t}^{k})\|^{2}.

For the time-averaged quantities, for any
T>\tau
, define

\displaystyle M_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T-\tau}\sum_{t=\tau}^{T-1}M_{t},\qquad X_{T}^{k}=\frac{1}{T-\tau}\sum_{t=\tau}^{T-1}{\mathbb{E}}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2},\qquad G_{T}^{k}=\frac{1}{T-\tau}\sum_{t=\tau}^{T-1}{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{t}^{k})\|^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}X_{T}^{k}=\frac{1}{T-\tau}\sum_{t=\tau}^{T-1}\bar{X}_{t},\qquad\bar{G}_{T}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}G_{T}^{k}=\frac{1}{T-\tau}\sum_{t=\tau}^{T-1}\bar{G}_{t}.

Lemma 5.1

(Upper bound of local head errors (informal))
.

Let
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}={\mathbf{B}}^{*}({\mathbf{B}}^{*})^{\top}
, and
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}={\mathbf{B}}_{t}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}
for each
t
.
Choose the mixing window so that
\tau L=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
,
U_{\delta}U_{\omega}\zeta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, where
U_{\delta}=U_{r}+2U_{\omega}
,
\beta=c\zeta
, and
\alpha=c_{\theta}\zeta
for arbitrary
c,c_{\theta}>0
that can be tuned. For any positive constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
, there exist constants
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})
such that, for all
t\geq\tau
,

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{t+1}\leq\Bigg(1-2\lambda c\zeta+\left(c_{2}^{2}\frac{c}{L}+c_{3}^{2}\frac{1}{L}+\frac{4U_{\omega}^{4}}{c_{3}^{2}L}+4BL_{*,1}c_{\theta}+c_{4}^{2}c_{\theta}\right)\zeta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{864U_{\delta}U_{\omega}^{3}c}{c_{1}^{2}}\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L^{3}(1-\gamma)}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\zeta^{2}\Bigg)\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{36U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{2}^{2}}\frac{c\zeta}{L}+6U_{\omega}U_{\delta}c\frac{\zeta\left(1+c_{1}^{2}\zeta\right)}{L(1-\gamma)}\right)M_{t}+\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)^{2}L_{*,1}^{2}c_{\theta}}{c_{4}^{2}}\zeta+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\zeta^{2}\right)\bar{G}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L}+\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K}\right)+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\zeta^{3}+8c_{\theta}U_{\omega}L_{*,1}U_{\delta}B\,\zeta\,\gamma^{\tau L}.

The constants are defined in (
E
)

Intuitively, when
M_{t}\lesssim\bar{X}_{t}
, the above upper bound is similar to the single-agent setting
6
.

Deriving Lemma
5.1
is highly nontrivial and requires a fundamental detour from the existing analysis
6
. Furthermore, existing analysis in PFL
8
is not applicable to our problem due to the Markovian sampling, the TD updates, and the lack of responses.

We further show that it is impossible for
M_{t}\gg\bar{X}_{t}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

43
[Lower bound of local head errors]

Suppose that
d\geq 2r
. It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}\geq\frac{\|m_{t}\|^{2}}{K}\lambda^{+}_{\min}({\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top})\geq\frac{\|m_{t}\|_{F}^{2}}{rK}\lambda^{+}_{\min}({\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*}{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}^{*\top}).

Importantly, Lemma
5.2
should not be interpreted as a problem-dependent lower bound characterizing the fundamental limits of a class of problems. Rather, it is an algorithm-specific intermediate result that plays a central role in our analysis. In particular, the lemma reveals that under Algorithm
1
, the quantities
M_{t}
and
X_{t}
are necessarily learned simultaneously throughout the training process.

Lemma 5.3

(Principal angle distance)
.

Choosing
\beta=c\zeta
and
\alpha=c_{\theta}\zeta
for some fixed
c,c_{\theta}>0
, and a mixing window satisfying
\tau L=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
, suppose that
d\geq 2r
,
L,K\geq 1
, and

\zeta\leq\min\left\{1,\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}U_{\delta}U_{\omega}},\frac{\lambda\nu}{3rU_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}},\frac{L(1-\gamma)}{2U_{\delta}U_{\omega}},\frac{1}{2U_{\omega}}\right\}.

Then, with the notation introduced above, for any
c_{1}>0
, there exist constants
{\mathcal{C}}_{M,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{M,2}(\tau^{2};c,c_{\theta})
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{M,3}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})
such that, for all
t\geq\tau
,

\displaystyle M_{t+1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\lambda\nu\zeta}{r}+c_{1}^{2}\zeta\right)M_{t}+\left(\frac{144U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{1}^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{L^{2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{M,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L^{2}}\right)\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{M,2}(\tau^{2};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{3}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L}+\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K}\right)+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{M,3}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\zeta^{3}.

The constants are defined in (
F
).

Lemma 5.4

(Policy gradient analysis)
.

Choosing
\beta=c\zeta
and
\alpha=c_{\theta}\zeta
for some fixed

c,c_{\theta}>0
, and the mixing window satisfies
\tau L=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
, suppose

\alpha\leq 1
and
16L_{J^{\prime}}\alpha\leq 1
. Then, with the notation
introduced above, for any
T>\tau
there exist positive constants

{\mathcal{C}}_{G,0},\ldots,{\mathcal{C}}_{G,11}
such that

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{T}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,0}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}c_{\theta}\zeta(T-\tau)}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,1}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\bar{X}_{T}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,2}\tau^{2}c_{\theta}^{2}\zeta^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,3}\tau^{2}cc_{\theta}\zeta^{2}}{L(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,4}\tau^{2}c_{\theta}\zeta^{2}}{L(1-\gamma)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,5}\tau c_{\theta}\zeta}{(1-\gamma)\sqrt{L(1-\gamma)}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,6}\tau^{2}c^{2}c_{\theta}\zeta^{3}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,7}\tau^{2}c_{\theta}\zeta^{3}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,8}\tau^{2}c_{\theta}^{3}\zeta^{3}}{1-\gamma}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,9}\tau c_{\theta}^{2}\zeta^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{3}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,10}c_{\theta}\zeta}{L(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{G,11}\gamma^{\tau L}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}.

The constants are defined in (
G
).

Theorem 1

(Convergence (informal))
.

Consider Algorithm
1
with
\beta=c\zeta
and

\alpha=c_{\theta}\zeta
, where
c,c_{\theta}>0
are fixed constants satisfying the
feasibility conditions in the proof. Choose
\zeta=\frac{L^{1/4}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Let
\tau L=\max\{\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil,\lceil 2\log_{\gamma}(\zeta)\rceil\}.

If
T
is sufficiently large to satisfy the small-stepsize conditions in the
proof, then

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{4}L^{1/4}\sqrt{T}}+\frac{L^{1/4}}{(1-\gamma)^{4}K\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\sqrt{L}}{(1-\gamma)^{5}T}\right),

\displaystyle\bar{G}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{6}L^{1/4}\sqrt{T}}+\frac{L^{1/4}}{(1-\gamma)^{6}K\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\sqrt{L}}{(1-\gamma)^{7}T}\right).

Taking
L=K^{2}
and assuming that the higher-order term is
dominated gives

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{T}

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{4}\sqrt{TK}}\right),\qquad\bar{G}_{T}\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}\left(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{6}\sqrt{TK}}\right).

Here
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}
hides logarithmic factors and the Lipschitz constants

L_{*,1}
,
L_{s,1}
, and
L_{J^{\prime}}
.

This gives the standard linear speedup for nonconvex stochastic optimization: the dominant policy-gradient term scales as
\widetilde{{\mathcal{O}}}(1/\sqrt{TK})
.

6
Experiments

We further instantiate our shared-representation and personalized-head structure within PPO
32
, yielding FedPer PPO, and evaluate it against Single PPO and FedAvg PPO on a federated
Hopper-v5
benchmark. In this benchmark, clients share the same underlying dynamics but receive distinct
action maps
.
Appendix
J
gives the full construction. All results are env-step-fair: we report the rolling-100 episode return at
0.4\,\text{M}
per-client environment steps, with means
\pm
standard deviations over
3
seeds.

6.1
Personalization benefits most under fully-distinct heterogeneity

The grouped setup allows FedAvg’s averaged parameters to specialize to two shared action maps, whereas the fully distinct setup forces FedAvg to reconcile six conflicting permutations, leading to a substantial performance drop. In contrast, FedPer’s shared trunk can no longer specialize to a small number of maps and is therefore forced to learn an action-mapping-invariant representation that supports all six interfaces. At the fair budget, the ratios are
\text{FedPer}/\text{Single}=2.63\times
and
\text{FedPer}/\text{FedAvg}=3.78\times
in
6-unique
, compared with
2.04\times
and
1.40\times
in the grouped setup.

Figure 1
:
Same six clients under two action-map heterogeneity structures: grouped, where three clients share each action map, and
6-unique
, where every client has a distinct action map.

Thus, personalization with a shared trunk yields its largest gain precisely in the regime where direct parameter averaging becomes unreliable.

6.2
The FedPer trunk is a transferable foundation model

Figure 2
:
Foundation transfer. The trunk is loaded from a single FedPer
6-unique
checkpoint; the actor and critic output heads are randomly re-initialized.
frozen
trains only the new output heads (
1{,}031/69{,}895
parameters);
trainable
fine-tunes the entire warm-started network;
scratch
trains a single PPO model from scratch. The dashed line marks the
0.4\,\text{M}
reporting budget.

The
6-unique
result in Section
6.1
suggests that the shared trunk learns a representation that is largely invariant to the action interface, which is the property that allows each client’s personalized head to specialize successfully. If this representation is reusable beyond the federated training tasks, it should accelerate learning on downstream tasks that were never optimized during federation, even when the pretrained trunk is frozen and only a tiny fraction of the network, namely the randomly initialized heads, is trained. We test this on two single-agent settings: (i)
vanilla
Hopper with no action remapping, and (ii) an
out-of-distribution (OOD)
variant using an action scale below the entire pretraining range, so that the trunk has not seen this interface during federation. At
0.4\,\text{M}
env-steps,
frozen
attains
5.42\times

scratch
on vanilla (
1328\pm 74
vs.
245\pm 45
) and
3.50\times
on OOD (
701\pm 101
vs.
200\pm 23
). The OOD result indicates out-of-distribution transfer, and
frozen
exceeds
trainable
on both tasks (
5.42\!>\!3.44\times
vanilla,
3.50\!>\!2.21\times
OOD), suggesting that on-policy fine-tuning can disrupt the pretrained representation before the randomly initialized heads recover. Additional results are provided in Appendix
K
.

7
Conclusion and Discussions

In this paper, we studied collaborative yet personalized policy training in heterogeneous environments through a federated single-timescale actor–critic framework. By leveraging a shared linear subspace representation while maintaining personalized local policy components, our approach enables agents to benefit from collaboration without sacrificing adaptability to local environments. We established finite-time convergence guarantees under Markovian sampling and heterogeneous transition dynamics.

Several important directions remain open for future work. In particular, improving the dependence of the convergence rates on the discount factor
(1-\gamma)^{-1}
remains a key challenge, especially in the near-undiscounted regime where
\gamma\to 1
. We believe sharper analyses that better exploit the underlying Markovian structure, variance reduction techniques, or refined stability properties of actor–critic dynamics may lead to significantly improved bounds.

Appendices

Appendix A
Limitations

See Section
7
.

Appendix B
Broader Impacts

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of Machine Learning. There are many potential societal consequences of our work, none of which we feel must be specifically highlighted here.

Appendix C
Related Work

MARL under homogeneous environments.

When the environments of all agents are homogeneous, it has been shown that the federated version of classic reinforcement learning algorithms can significantly alleviate the data collection burden on individual agents
[
45
,
19
]
. Specifically, the per-agent sample complexity decreases linearly in terms of the number of agents – an effect commonly referred to in existing literature as linear speedup.
Recent work has made significant progress in understanding federated RL under homogeneous environments.

[
45
]
studied federated Q-learning.
In addition to a linear speedup,
[
45
]
uncovered the blessing of heterogeneity in terms of state-action exploration – a completely different notion of heterogeneity from our focus.

[
31
]
studied the sample-communication complexity trade-off, and developed a new algorithm that achieves order-optimal sample and communication complexities.

Model/Policy personalization.

In FL, model personalization (often referred to as PFL) has garnered significant attention in recent years.
The success of personalization techniques depends on balancing the bias introduced by using global knowledge that may not generalize to individual clients, and the variance inherent in learning from limited local datasets. Popular PFL techniques include regularized local objectives
[
36
,
23
]
, local-global parameter interpolation
[
10
]
, meta-learning
[
13
,
16
]
, and representation learning
[
8
,
50
]
.
Extending these ideas of PFL to RL introduces an even greater level of difficulty that arises from the inherent sample correlation induced by sequential decision-making, and the non-stationarity of sample quality as the policy evolves.

In the RL literature, training personalized (or localized) policies has recently garnered considerable attention in the context of multi-agent Markov games or competitive MARL
[
14
,
55
,
30
,
56
,
9
]
, which depart fundamentally from our focus.
Specifically, in their setting, agents interact with one another within a shared environment, and the rewards received by individual agents are determined by the joint actions of all agents. Each agent aims to learn a localized policy that contributes to a joint policy across all agents, with the goal of maximizing its own welfare.
A commonly adopted objective among the participating agents is to learn some form of equilibrium, such as a Nash Equilibrium.
Departing from Markov games, we focus on cooperative MARL, where we try to avoid the curse of heterogeneity in the joint learning.

Federated RL and actor-critic under heterogeneity.

Federated RL under heterogeneous environments or tasks has recently attracted increasing attention.
Existing theoretical studies include value-based control methods such as federated Q-learning and SARSA, as well as critic-only TD learning with linear function approximation
[
18
,
44
,
42
,
53
]
.
These value-based or critic-only methods are distinct from the policy-gradient family: Q-learning and SARSA learn action-value functions for control, while TD learning focuses on policy evaluation or critic estimation.
Policy-gradient methods have also been studied in heterogeneous federated RL; for example,
[
41
]
developed momentum-based federated policy-gradient methods, and
[
21
]
studied global convergence rates of federated softmax policy gradient, both aiming to learn a common policy that optimizes an average objective across heterogeneous environments.
Federated actor-critic methods form the most directly related line of work.
[
51
]
studied federated natural policy-gradient and actor-critic methods for multi-task reinforcement learning, where agents collaborate to learn a shared policy under task heterogeneity.
More recently,
[
57
]
analyzed a single-loop federated actor-critic method for learning a shared policy across heterogeneous environments. Their “single-loop” terminology refers to preserving the critic across policy updates, while the actor is still updated after multiple critic communication rounds.

[
48
]
studied that, in the setting of FRL, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) suffers from data heterogeneity when local actors are updated based on local critic estimates, and they proposed FedRAC to mitigate this problem.

Despite these advances, existing federated reinforcement learning analyses mainly focus on learning a common policy, a common value function, or a shared objective across agents.
In contrast, our goal is to learn personalized policies under heterogeneous transition kernels, where agents share a low-dimensional representation while maintaining agent-specific local heads.
This personalization structure introduces additional coupled dynamics among policy updates, local heads, shared representation, and Markovian sampling errors, which are not captured by existing federated RL or federated actor-critic analyses.

Appendix D
Notations and Preliminary Results

D.1
Auxiliary Markov chains for critic and actor

Following
[
6
,
7
]
, our finite-time analysis leverages two auxiliary Markov chains that are tightly coupled with the critic and actor Markov chains, respectively. The key distinction from the original chains is that, in both auxiliary chains, the policy parameter
\theta^{k}
is frozen over the most recent
\tau
rounds. Note that both auxiliary chains are purely hypothetical constructs introduced for analysis purposes and do not incur any additional sampling complexity.

Let
v_{0:t}^{k}
denote the Markovian trajectories up to time
t
generated under initial state distribution
\eta
, the local transition kernel
P^{k}
, the critic and actor updates in Algorithm
1
:

\displaystyle v_{0:t}^{k}=(s_{0}^{k},\widehat{s}_{0}^{k},~\{s_{0,0}^{k},a_{0,0}^{k},\cdots,s_{0,L}^{k}\},\{\widehat{s}_{0,0}^{k},\widehat{a}_{0,0}^{k},

\displaystyle\cdots,\widehat{s}_{0,L}^{k}\},\cdots,

\displaystyle\qquad~\{s_{t,0}^{k},a_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,s_{t,L}^{k}\},\{\widehat{s}_{t,0}^{k},\widehat{a}_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,\widehat{s}_{t,L}^{k}\})

with the understanding that
s_{0,0}^{k}=s_{0}^{k}
,
\widehat{s}_{0,0}^{k}=\widehat{s}_{0}^{k}
,
s_{r-1,L}^{k}=s_{r,0}^{k}
, and
\widehat{s}_{r-1,L}^{k}=\widehat{s}_{r,0}^{k}
for
1\leq r\leq t
.
Let
v_{0:t}
denote the collection of Markovian trajectories generated by all agents:

\displaystyle v_{0:t}=\{v_{0:t}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}.

Similarly, we define
v_{0:(t,\ell)}^{k}
and
v_{0:(t,\ell)}
as the Markovian trajectory generated up to the
\ell
-th sample in time
t
.
For ease of exposition, let
tr_{t}^{k}=\{s_{t,0}^{k},a_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,s_{t,L}^{k}\}
and
\widehat{tr}_{t}^{k}=\{\widehat{s}_{t,0}^{k},\widehat{a}_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,\widehat{s}_{t,L}^{k}\}
.

Furthermore, we note that for the original critic and actor Markov chains, the policy parameter
\theta^{k}
changes each round but remains fixed within a round. In contrast, for the auxiliary Markov chains associated with round
t
, the policy is last updated at round
t-\tau
and is thereafter held fixed through round
t
.
That is, with a little abuse of notation,

\displaystyle s_{0}^{k}\xrightarrow{\theta_{0}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{0}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{0}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{0}^{k}\xrightarrow{\theta_{1}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{1}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{t-\tau}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{t-\tau}^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\xrightarrow{\theta_{t-\tau+1}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t-\tau+1}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{t-\tau+1}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{t-\tau+1}^{k}\cdots\xrightarrow{\theta_{t}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{t}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{t}^{k}.

We define the auxiliary Markov chains:

\displaystyle s_{0}^{k}\xrightarrow{\theta_{0}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{0}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{0}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{0}^{k}\xrightarrow{\theta_{1}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{1}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}tr_{t-\tau}^{k},\widehat{tr}_{t-\tau}^{k}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\xrightarrow{\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}\widetilde{t}r_{t-\tau+1}^{k},\bar{tr}_{t-\tau+1}^{k}\cdots\xrightarrow{\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times P^{k},\theta_{t-\tau}^{k}\times\widehat{P}^{k}}\widetilde{tr}_{t}^{k},\bar{tr}_{t}^{k},

where for
t-\tau<r\leq t
,

\widetilde{tr}_{t}^{k}=\{\widetilde{s}_{t,0}^{k},\widetilde{a}_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,\widetilde{s}_{t,L}^{k}\}
and
\bar{tr}_{t}^{k}=\{\bar{s}_{t,0}^{k},\bar{a}_{t,0}^{k},\cdots,\bar{s}_{t,L}^{k}\}
.

To simplify notation, we use dots to indicate different levels of marginalization:

For critic updates:

•

{\mathbb{P}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot)
/
{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot)
denotes the marginal probability distribution over states
s\in\mathcal{S}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of critic.

•

{\mathbb{P}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
/
{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the marginal probability distribution over state-action pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of critic.

•

{\mathbb{P}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
/
{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the full joint probability distribution over tuple
(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of critic.

{\mathbb{P}}_{t-\tau:(t,\ell)}^{k}
and
{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:(t,\ell)}^{k}
are defined analogously for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of critic.

For actor updates:

•

\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot)
/
\bar{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot)
denotes the marginal probability distribution over states
s\in\mathcal{S}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of actor.

•

\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
/
\bar{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the marginal probability distribution over state-action pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of actor.

•

\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
/
\bar{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:t}^{k}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the full joint probability distribution over tuple
(s,a,s^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{S}
at time
t
given the past trajectory
v_{0:t-\tau}
for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of actor.

\widehat{{\mathbb{P}}}_{t-\tau:(t,\ell)}^{k}
and
\bar{\mathbb{U}}_{t-\tau:(t,\ell)}^{k}
are defined analogously for the original/auxiliary Markov chain of the actor.

For any
j\leq t
, let
{\mathbb{E}}_{j}[X_{t}]
denote the conditional expectation of any random variable
X_{t}
given the collection of Markovian trajectories up to time
j
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}[X_{t}]={\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{t}\mid v_{0:j}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{t}\mid\{v_{0:j}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\right].

When
j=t
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{j}[X_{t}]={\mathbb{E}}[X_{t}]
is the full expectation.

For ease of exposition, for any given
\theta
, we occasionally abbreviate
\mathbb{E}_{s^{(0)}\sim\mu_{\theta}^{k},(a^{(\ell)},s^{(\ell+1)})\sim\pi_{\theta}\otimes P^{k}~\forall\ell\in[0,L-1]}
as
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\theta}^{k},\pi_{\theta},P^{k}}
.

D.2
Boundedness and TD Features Decomposition

Let

\displaystyle\xi_{t,L}^{k}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r_{t,\ell}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})+(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})

(16)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k},\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}},P^{k}}\left[\gamma^{\ell}r(s^{(\ell)},a^{(\ell)})\phi(s^{(0)})\right]-{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k},\pi_{\theta_{t}^{k}},P^{k}}\left[(\gamma^{L}\phi(s^{(L)})-\phi(s^{(0)}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}\phi(s^{(0)})\right],

denote the Markovian noise of the TD(
L
) error of agent
k
at time
t
.

Denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r_{t,\ell}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}),~~~

(17)

\displaystyle\bar{b}_{L,\theta}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\mu_{\theta}^{k},\pi_{\theta},P^{k}}\left[\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r(s^{(\ell)},a^{(\ell)})\phi(s^{(0)})\right],~~~

(18)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}

\displaystyle=\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top},~~~

(19)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}\big(r_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma\phi(s_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,\ell}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}^{*}\omega_{t}^{k,*}\big)\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}).~~~

(20)

The expression of
\xi_{t,L}^{k}
can be simplified as

\displaystyle\xi_{t,L}^{k}=\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}-\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}+\left(\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}-A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\right){\mathbf{B}}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}.

(21)

The following rewrites of the intermediate quantitiy
\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t}^{k})
, which we refer to as TD feature, appear repeatedly and alternatively in our analysis.
They correspond to two different ways of separating the Markovian noise from the value function estimation error
x_{t}^{k}
as per defined in Eq.(
13
).

Proposition 2

(TD feature decomposition)
.

For any
t
and agent
k
,
\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t}^{k})=\xi_{t,L}^{k}+A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}x_{t}^{k},

(22)

and

\displaystyle\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t}^{k})=\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}+{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}.

(23)

The proof of Proposition
2
can be found in Appendix
I.1
.

Lemma D.1
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
holds.
Define
U_{\delta}:=U_{r}+2U_{\omega}.

For any
t\geq 0,L\geq 0,k\in[K]
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\delta^{k}_{t,L}|\leq\frac{U_{\delta}}{1-\gamma},\quad\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad|\delta_{i,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}|\leq U_{\delta}~~\forall~\ell=0,\cdots,L-1

(24)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|\leq\frac{U_{\delta}}{1-\gamma},

(25)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}-\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|\leq\frac{2U_{r}}{1-\gamma},\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad~~{\mathbb{E}}\|\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}-\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4U_{r}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}},

(26)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}-A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}}\right\|\leq 4\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad{\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}-A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}}\right\|^{2}\leq 16,

(27)

where the expectation is taken over the Markovian sampling trajectories.

Proof.

From Eq. (
7
), we have

\displaystyle|\delta^{k}_{t,L}|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}\left(r_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma\phi(s_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,\ell}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U_{r}+2U_{\omega}}{1-\gamma}=\frac{U_{\delta}}{1-\gamma}.

where the last inequality holds due to
\|\gamma^{L}\phi(s^{k}_{t+1})-\phi(s^{k}_{t})\|\leq 2
under Assumption
4
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle|\delta_{t,\ell}^{k,\mathrm{act}}|

\displaystyle\leq|\widehat{r}_{t,\ell}^{k}|+|(\gamma\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell+1}^{k})-\phi(\widehat{s}_{t,\ell}^{k}))^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{t}\omega_{t}^{k}|

\displaystyle\leq U_{r}+2U_{\omega}=U_{\delta},~~~\forall~\ell=0,\ldots,L-1.

From Eq.(
20
), we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=\|\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r_{t,\ell}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})+(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}z_{t}^{k,*}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}r_{t,\ell}^{k}+(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}z_{t}^{k,*}\|~~~~\text{by Assumption \ref{ass: bounded features}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U_{r}+2U_{\omega}}{1-\gamma}=\frac{U_{\delta}}{1-\gamma}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}\|\leq\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\gamma^{\ell}|r_{t,\ell}^{k}|\|\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|\leq\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma},\qquad\text{and}~\qquad\|\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|\leq\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma},

resulting in

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}-\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|\leq\frac{2U_{r}}{1-\gamma},\qquad\|\widetilde{b}_{t,L}^{k}-\bar{b}_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4U_{r}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}.

Furthermore, by Assumption
4
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|\|(\gamma^{L}\phi(s_{t,L}^{k})-\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))^{\top}\|\leq 1+\gamma^{L}\leq 2,

\displaystyle\|A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\|\phi(s^{(0)})(\gamma^{L}\phi(s^{(L)})-\phi(s^{(0)}))^{\top}\|\leq 2.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}-A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|\leq 4,\qquad\|\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}-A_{L,\theta_{t}^{k}}^{k}\|^{2}\leq 16.

∎

D.3
Bound on QR Decomposition and Perturbation

Recall from Algorithm
1
the update of
\bar{\mathbf{B}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}=\mathbf{B}_{t}+\frac{\zeta}{KL}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}

For ease of exposition, let

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}:=\frac{\zeta}{KL}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}.

(28)

The following couple of lemmas enable us to bound the “distortion” caused by the QR decomposition.
Specifically, Lemma
D.2
bounds the perturbation in terms of
\|\bf Q_{t}\|
, which is further bounded in Lemma
D.3
.
It is worth noting that, in the traditional PFL
[
8
]
, such distortion can be well controlled by the number of
i.i.d.
local samples that are freshly drawn in each iteration. However, their analysis does not extend to our setting due to the temporal dependence induced by Markovian sampling. In contrast to
[
8
]
, a recent work derived upper bounds on these distortions through a novel fixed-point iteration argument. Notably, the results in
[
43
]
were established for average reward. Their analysis can be easily extended to the discounted setting.

Lemma D.2

(Perturbation of QR)
.

[
43
]

For each
t\geq 1
, when
\frac{U_{\delta}U_{\omega}}{L(1-\gamma)}\zeta\leq 1/2
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|{{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}}\|_{2}\leq 2\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2},

(29)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{1-\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\|_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{1-2\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}},

(30)

\displaystyle\|{{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}^{-1}-{\mathbf{I}}}\|_{2}\leq 4\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}.

(31)

Lemma D.3
.

For any
t\geq\tau
, when
\frac{U_{\delta}U_{\omega}}{L(1-\gamma)}\zeta\leq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Q}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{U_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}}\zeta^{2}.~

(32)

Proof.

For any
t\geq\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{\zeta}{KL}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}{\mathbf{B}}_{t,\perp}^{\top}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|\frac{\zeta}{KL}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K^{2}L^{2}}K\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})(\omega_{t}^{k})^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{\zeta^{2}}{KL^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}\|^{2}\|\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}\|\omega_{t}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}}\zeta^{2},

where the last inequality follows from Assumption
4
and Lemma
D.1
.

∎

D.4
Lipschitz Bounds for Policy-Parameter Perturbations

Lemma D.4
.

[
46
]

For any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, we have

|J^{k}(\theta_{1})-J^{k}(\theta_{2})|\leq L_{J}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,\text{ for }k\in[K]

where
L_{J}=2U_{r}|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(1+\frac{1}{1-\rho}).

Lemma D.5
.

[
54
]

For the performance function
J^{k}(\theta)
, there exists a constant
L_{J^{\prime}}>0
such that for all
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, it holds that

\|\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{1})-\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{2})\|\leq L_{J^{\prime}}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,\text{ for }k\in[K],

which further implies

J^{k}(\theta_{2})\geq J^{k}(\theta_{1})+\langle\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{1}),\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle-\frac{L_{J^{\prime}}}{2}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2},

J^{k}(\theta_{2})\leq J^{k}(\theta_{1})+\langle\nabla J^{k}(\theta_{1}),\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}\rangle+\frac{L_{J^{\prime}}}{2}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|^{2}.

Lemma D.6
.

There exists a constant
L_{*,1}>0
such that

\|\omega^{k,*}(\theta_{1},\mathbf{B}^{*})-\omega^{k,*}({\theta_{2}},\mathbf{B}^{*})\|\leq L_{*,1}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,\forall\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

where one admissible choice is

L_{*,1}=\frac{1}{\lambda L}\left(\frac{2U_{r}C_{\mu}}{1-\gamma}+\frac{U_{r}L_{\pi}|{\mathcal{A}}|}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\right)+\frac{U_{r}}{1-\gamma}(\lambda L)^{-2}\left(\frac{4C_{\mu}}{1-\gamma}+\frac{2L_{\pi}|{\mathcal{A}}|}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\right).

Lemma D.7
.

Assume that
\nabla_{\theta}\mu_{\theta}^{k}
and
\nabla_{\theta}\nu_{\theta}^{k}
exist and are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz in
\theta
for every
k\in[K]
.
For any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},
we have

\|\nabla\omega^{k,*}(\theta_{1},\mathbf{B}^{*})-\nabla\omega^{k,*}(\theta_{2},\mathbf{B}^{*})\|\leq L_{s,1}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

where
L_{s,1}
is a positive constant.

Lemma D.8
.

For any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, it holds that

d_{TV}(\mu^{k}_{\theta_{1}},\mu^{k}_{\theta_{2}})\leq\underbrace{|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(\frac{1}{1-\rho})}_{:=C_{\mu}}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

d_{TV}(\mu^{k}_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{1}},\mu^{k}_{\theta_{2}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{2}})\leq\underbrace{|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(1+\frac{1}{1-\rho})}_{:=C_{\mu,\pi}}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

d_{TV}(\mu^{k}_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{1}}\otimes P^{k},\mu^{k}_{\theta_{2}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{2}}\otimes P^{k})\leq|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(1+\frac{1}{1-\rho})\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|.

Lemmas
D.6
,
D.7
, and
D.8
are proved in Appendix
I.5
.
Following the same line of analysis as in the proof of Lemma
D.8
, the following holds.

Corollary 1
.

For any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, it holds that

d_{TV}(\nu^{k}_{\theta_{1}},\nu^{k}_{\theta_{2}})\leq\underbrace{|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(\frac{1}{1-\gamma})}_{:=C_{\nu}}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

d_{TV}(\nu^{k}_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{1}},\nu^{k}_{\theta_{2}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{2}})\leq\underbrace{|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(1+\frac{1}{1-\gamma})}_{:=C_{\nu,\pi}}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|,

d_{TV}(\nu^{k}_{\theta_{1}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{1}}\otimes P^{k},\nu^{k}_{\theta_{2}}\otimes\pi_{\theta_{2}}\otimes P^{k})\leq|{\mathcal{A}}|L_{\pi}(1+\frac{1}{1-\gamma})\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma
5.1
(Bounds on Local Head Errors)

We first provide a formal statement of Lemma
5.1
, which involves a set of constants whose formal definitions are deferred to the lemma proof.

Lemma E.1

(Upper bound of local head errors)
.

Choose
\beta
such that
\frac{U_{\delta}}{L(1-\gamma)}\beta\leq U_{\omega}
.
Let
{\mathbf{P}}^{*}={\mathbf{B}}^{*}({\mathbf{B}}^{*})^{\top}
, and
{\mathbf{P}}_{t}={\mathbf{B}}_{t}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}
for each
t
.
Choose the mixing window so that
\tau L=\lceil 2\log_{\rho}(\zeta)\rceil
,
U_{\delta}U_{\omega}\zeta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, where
U_{\delta}=U_{r}+2U_{\omega}
,
\beta=c\zeta
, and
\alpha=c_{\theta}\zeta
for arbitrary
c,c_{\theta}>0
that can be tuned. For any positive constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
, there exist constants
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})
, and
{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})
such that, for all
t\geq\tau
,

\displaystyle\bar{X}_{t+1}\leq\Bigg(1-2\lambda c\zeta+\left(c_{2}^{2}\frac{c}{L}+c_{3}^{2}\frac{1}{L}+\frac{4U_{\omega}^{4}}{c_{3}^{2}L}+4BL_{*,1}c_{\theta}+c_{4}^{2}c_{\theta}\right)\zeta

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{864U_{\delta}U_{\omega}^{3}c}{c_{1}^{2}}\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L^{3}(1-\gamma)}+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,1}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\zeta^{2}\Bigg)\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{36U_{\omega}^{2}}{c_{2}^{2}}\frac{c\zeta}{L}+6U_{\omega}U_{\delta}c\frac{\zeta}{L(1-\gamma)}\left(1+c_{1}^{2}\zeta\right)\right)M_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)^{2}L_{*,1}^{2}c_{\theta}}{c_{4}^{2}}\zeta+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,2}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}\zeta^{2}\right)\bar{G}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,3}(\tau^{4};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\left(\frac{\zeta^{2}}{\sqrt{L}}+\frac{\zeta^{2}}{L}+\frac{\zeta^{2}}{K}\right)+\frac{{\mathcal{C}}_{X,4}(\tau^{6};c,c_{\theta})}{(1-\gamma)^{4}}\zeta^{3}+8c_{\theta}U_{\omega}L_{*,1}U_{\delta}B\,\zeta\,\gamma^{\tau L}.

Proof.

Recall that
\omega_{t+1}^{k}=\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\omega_{t}^{k}+\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k}))
,
{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}^{-1}
, and
\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}=\omega_{t}^{k}+\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})
.
We have

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}^{k}\|=

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-z_{t+1}^{k,*}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-z_{t+1}^{k,*}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-z_{t+1}^{k,*}+{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-z_{t+1}^{k,*}+{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}+{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|.

Taking the square on both sides, we get

\displaystyle\|x_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}

(33)

\displaystyle+2\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}\rangle.

We bound
\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}
as follows:

We have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}+\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|+\|({\mathbf{I}}-{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1})\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|+\|({\mathbf{I}}-{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1})\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|+4U_{\omega}\|{\mathbf{Q}}\|_{F}^{2},

where the last inequality follows from Eq. (
29
) and we use
\frac{U_{\delta}}{L(1-\gamma)}\beta\leq U_{\omega}
such that
\|\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|\leq 2U_{\omega}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}+32U_{\omega}^{2}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4}.

For the first term, we further decompose it as:

\displaystyle\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}\leq\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}+{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|^{2}~~~~(\text{by Proposition \ref{prop: key intermediate}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\|\widetilde{A}_{t,L}^{k}x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+2\|{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+2\|{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|^{2}.

Taking the conditional expectation over the Markovian trajectory up to
t-\tau
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+2{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{b}}_{t,L}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{2U_{\delta}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}.~~(\text{by Lemma \ref{lmm: U delta}})

(34)

Finally, the second term in Eq. (
33
) can be bounded as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 16\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+4\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\frac{U_{\delta}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+32U_{\omega}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4},

(35)

where
{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4}
can be bounded by invoking Lemma
D.3
.

As for
2\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}\rangle
,

we first write
\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}=\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{*,k}={\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}+\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},\widetilde{x}_{t+1}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}\rangle}_{I_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}\rangle}_{I_{2}}.

Both
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
can be well controlled by the projection error of
\widetilde{w}
and the perturbation error that arises from the QR decomposition of
\bar{{\mathbf{B}}}
.
We bound
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
separately.

On
I_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}+{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\right)+{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\left({\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\right)\omega_{t+1}^{k}.

So,
I_{2}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},\bar{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\right)\rangle}_{I_{21}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\left({\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\right)\omega_{t+1}^{k}\rangle}_{I_{22}}.

For
I_{21}
, by Cauchy Schwartz, we have

\displaystyle I_{21}\leq\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\right)\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\left(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\right)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+\|\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2},

where the last inequality holds because that
\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\|\leq 2
, which we establish next.
From Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
D.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\|\leq\|{\mathbf{I}}\|+\|{\mathbf{I}}-{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\|\leq 1+2\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 1+2\frac{U_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}\zeta^{2}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}}.

When
2\frac{U_{\delta}^{2}U_{\omega}^{2}}{L^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}}\zeta^{2}\leq 1/2
,
\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\|\leq 2
.
As for
\|\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}
, by (
E
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|\frac{\beta}{L}\delta_{t,L}^{k}{\mathbf{B}}_{t}^{\top}\phi(s_{t,0}^{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\frac{2U_{\delta}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}.

For
I_{22}
, we have

\displaystyle I_{22}\leq\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\left({\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\right)\omega_{t+1}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\left({\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\right)\omega_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}U_{\omega}^{2}\|{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}-{\mathbf{I}}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}+2U_{\omega}^{2}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4}.~~~~\text{(by \eqref{eqn: R related bounds: R-I})}

Combining
I_{21}
and
I_{22}
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}[I_{2}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+8\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\frac{2U_{\delta}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+2U_{\omega}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 24\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|x_{t}^{k}\|^{2}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{L^{2}}\frac{6U_{\delta}^{2}}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}+34U_{\omega}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{t-\tau}\|{\mathbf{Q}}_{t}\|_{F}^{4}.~~~~~~\text{(by \eqref{eq: upper bound of qr-proj squared})}

Bounding
I_{1}
is is considerably more delicate and fundamentally differs from the analysis of non-personalized actor–critic methods. In particular, the errors induced by the projection operators associated with the local heads and the QR decomposition are tightly coupled. Without carefully controlling their interaction, the desired linear-speedup characterization cannot be established in the finite-time analysis. Towards this, we leverage geometric properties of projection operators and principal-angle distances.
We have

\displaystyle I_{1}=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\Pi_{U_{\omega}}(\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k})-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}\rangle}_{I_{11}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathbf{R}}_{t+1}\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k},{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}\rangle}_{I_{12}}

For
I_{11}
, let
{\mathcal{R}}={\mathbf{U}}{\mathbf{V}}^{\top},
where
{\mathbf{U}}\Sigma{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}={\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}^{\top}{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
is the SVD. Note that
{\mathcal{R}}
is the optimal rotation matrix that aligns
{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}
and
{\mathbf{B}}^{*}
in the sense that
{\mathcal{R}}=\text{argmin}_{{\mathcal{R}}^{\top}{\mathcal{R}}={\mathbf{I}}}\|{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\|_{F}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle I_{11}=\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}(\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}),{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}\omega_{t+1}^{k,*}\rangle~~~(\text{by rotation})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}),({\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}}-{\mathbf{B}}^{*}){\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}(\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}),{\mathbf{B}}^{*}({\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k,*})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}))^{\top}({\mathbf{I}}-({\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}})^{\top}{\mathbf{B}}^{*}){\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}

\displaystyle+\langle{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\widetilde{\omega}_{t+1}^{k}),({\mathbf{B}}^{*}-{\mathbf{B}}_{t+1}{\mathcal{R}})({\mathcal{R}}^{-1}\omega_{t+1}^{k}-\omega_{t+1}^{k,*})\rangle
